Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Yeah, um… I came in because I’m losing a lot of time rechecking things. Like dressing supplies, door locks, even file dimensions and revision notes at work—I’m an engineer, so that part kind of spirals because everything is supposed to be precise, and I’ll think, “Well, one more look won’t hurt,” but then it’s not one more.

I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops a bit. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because hours can disappear, and I know I should just leave it alone, but I get stuck.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… it’s less like I have one clear disaster in my head every time, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check. Like with the door lock, or my dressing supplies, or even file dimensions and revision notes at work—I’ll tell myself I just need to be sure, and then somehow I’m still there doing the same routine again.

Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yes, I guess the fear is that if I don’t check, something could be wrong, and I won’t be able to relax until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Hours can go that way.

It’s embarrassing, honestly, because hours can disappear, and I know I should just leave it alone, but I get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to check things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s less like I have one clear disaster in my head every time, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check. Like with the door lock, or my dressing supplies, or even file dimensions and revision notes at work—I’ll tell myself I just need to be sure, and then somehow I’m still there doing the same routine again.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m reviewing engineering drawings and you zoom in too far and suddenly every tiny line looks questionable, even if it was fine before. Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yes, I guess the fear is that if I don’t check, something could be wrong, and I won’t be able to relax until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Hours can go that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel the need to arrange things in a specific way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Um… arrange things in a specific way? I’m not sure. I don’t think that’s the main thing for me.

Like, I notice if something on my desk is crooked, but I mean, I’m an engineer, so half my day is lining things up on screens and making sure layers and dimensions aren’t weird. At home it’s more like… if my tools are scattered I get annoyed, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Yeah… yeah, that happens. Like I’ll be trying to work on something else, or even just move on with my day, and the thought cuts back in that I missed one tiny thing.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not always like I’m picturing one exact catastrophe, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’m leaving something unsafe or wrong.

Like with my dressing supplies, or the door locks, or work stuff—file dimensions, revision notes—I’ll think, okay, just confirm it once, and then somehow I’m back in the same loop. It’s almost like debugging, except there’s no actual error message, just this feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet.

Sorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, the checking is basically to prevent something bad, or to make the anxiety come down enough that I can leave it alone. And that can take hours sometimes.

I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like when things look neat, but I don’t think I need symmetry or that “just right” feeling to be comfortable.

It’s almost like debugging, except there’s no actual error message, just this feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, the checking is basically to prevent something bad, or to make the anxiety come down enough that I can leave it alone. And that can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… mostly with the wound area, I guess. Like even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll touch something or think I touched near it, and then this thought comes back that I missed one tiny spot or there’s still something there.

And then my brain kind of jumps ahead to, what if that turns into an infection? Even if I can tell myself, no, I already cleaned it, I already did the steps… it doesn’t really shut off.

It’s kind of similar to work, weirdly, like reviewing a CAD file and then thinking I missed one tiny design issue that could hurt someone. Sorry, that’s not exactly touching contamination, but it’s the same kind of “one small thing I missed” feeling.

Yeah, I do… though for me it kind of blends into checking, if that makes sense. It’s not just washing once and being done. I’ll clean or set out the dressing supplies, then I’ll think maybe I missed something, or touched the wrong part, and I end up going back through it again.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I’m losing a lot of time rechecking things. Like dressing supplies, door locks, even file dimensions and revision notes at work—I’m an engineer, so that part kind of spirals because everything is supposed to be precise, and I’ll think, “Well, one more look won’t hurt,” but then it’s not one more.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops a bit. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because hours can disappear, and I know I should just leave it alone, but I get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to check things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s less like I have one clear disaster in my head every time, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check. Like with the door lock, or my dressing supplies, or even file dimensions and revision notes at work—I’ll tell myself I just need to be sure, and then somehow I’m still there doing the same routine again.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m reviewing engineering drawings and you zoom in too far and suddenly every tiny line looks questionable, even if it was fine before. Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yes, I guess the fear is that if I don’t check, something could be wrong, and I won’t be able to relax until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Hours can go that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a specific way?\n\nPatient: Um… arrange things in a specific way? I’m not sure. I don’t think that’s the main thing for me.\n\nLike, I notice if something on my desk is crooked, but I mean, I’m an engineer, so half my day is lining things up on screens and making sure layers and dimensions aren’t weird. At home it’s more like… if my tools are scattered I get annoyed, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. My roommate once joked that I alphabetized cables, which I didn’t—well, I sort of labeled them, but that was because we kept losing the HDMI one.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed needing to arrange things in a special way, not in the same way as the checking stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. Like I’ll be trying to work on something else, or even just move on with my day, and the thought cuts back in that I missed one tiny thing.\n\nIt’s usually after I already know I cleaned the wound area, or I already reviewed the CAD file, but then my brain goes, what if there’s still something there? What if that turns into an infection, or what if that little design mistake ends up hurting someone? And then I’m not really focused anymore—I’m just stuck on that image or possibility.\n\nIt’s annoying because I can sort of explain to myself that I already checked it, but it doesn’t actually make the thought leave. It’s like… I can reason it through, but it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not always like I’m picturing one exact catastrophe, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’m leaving something unsafe or wrong.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, or the door locks, or work stuff—file dimensions, revision notes—I’ll think, okay, just confirm it once, and then somehow I’m back in the same loop. It’s almost like debugging, except there’s no actual error message, just this feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, the checking is basically to prevent something bad, or to make the anxiety come down enough that I can leave it alone. And that can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like when things look neat, but I don’t think I need symmetry or that “just right” feeling to be comfortable.\n\nLike, if a picture frame is crooked I might notice it, but then again I notice weird alignment in CAD drawings all day, so it’s hard to tell what’s just habit. My apartment isn’t, like, perfectly arranged or anything. My desk gets messy. I’ll straighten something sometimes, but I don’t feel stuck on it the way I… sorry, I’m kind of drifting.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that something you touch might be contaminated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with the wound area, I guess. Like even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll touch something or think I touched near it, and then this thought comes back that I missed one tiny spot or there’s still something there.\n\nAnd then my brain kind of jumps ahead to, what if that turns into an infection? Even if I can tell myself, no, I already cleaned it, I already did the steps… it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nIt’s kind of similar to work, weirdly, like reviewing a CAD file and then thinking I missed one tiny design issue that could hurt someone. Sorry, that’s not exactly touching contamination, but it’s the same kind of “one small thing I missed” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself washing your hands or cleaning things more than you think is necessary?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… though for me it kind of blends into checking, if that makes sense. It’s not just washing once and being done. I’ll clean or set out the dressing supplies, then I’ll think maybe I missed something, or touched the wrong part, and I end up going back through it again.\n\nAnd then it spreads to other things too—locks, work files, dimensions, revision notes. Sorry, that’s not exactly handwashing, but it’s the same pattern. I lose hours rechecking things because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… definitely. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in.

I keep going back over the same things—like the dressing supplies, making sure I laid everything out right, then checking again because I’m not sure if I touched something wrong. And door locks too. Yeah, I do. It’s usually not like a picture exactly, more like a thought that cuts in and won’t leave.

Like with the wound area, even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot and then it’s going to turn into an infection. And I try to tell myself that doesn’t make sense, or that I already did it, but it doesn’t really stick.

I know I’m probably going off into work stuff again, sorry, but it feels like the same thing. I try to push it away or reason through it, and it just kind of comes back louder.

Yeah… it is. I kept thinking it was just me being careful, especially with engineering stuff, because you’re supposed to check your work, right? Like, nobody wants to be the person who misses some tiny dimension and then it causes a whole chain of problems.

I don’t think it’s really about symmetry, exactly. It’s more like… exactness maybe, but not in a neatness way.

Sorry, that’s not exactly handwashing, but it’s the same pattern. I lose hours rechecking things because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like I’m scared there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and then I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet.

So I keep going back over things—my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not always that I find something wrong. Most of the time I don’t. But I’ll still lose hours because I’m trying to get to that point where my anxiety drops enough and it feels… finished.

And then I’ll start thinking, like, at work they always tell you to be thorough, and in engineering that’s kind of drilled into you, but this feels past that. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.

Like I’ll be trying to work on something else, or even just move on with my day, and the thought cuts back in that I missed one tiny thing.\n\nIt’s usually after I already know I cleaned the wound area, or I already reviewed the CAD file, but then my brain goes, what if there’s still something there? What if that turns into an infection, or what if that little design mistake ends up hurting someone? And then I’m not really focused anymore—I’m just stuck on that image or possibility.\n\nIt’s annoying because I can sort of explain to myself that I already checked it, but it doesn’t actually make the thought leave. It’s like… I can reason it through, but it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not always like I’m picturing one exact catastrophe, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’m leaving something unsafe or wrong.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, or the door locks, or work stuff—file dimensions, revision notes—I’ll think, okay, just confirm it once, and then somehow I’m back in the same loop. It’s almost like debugging, except there’s no actual error message, just this feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, the checking is basically to prevent something bad, or to make the anxiety come down enough that I can leave it alone. And that can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like when things look neat, but I don’t think I need symmetry or that “just right” feeling to be comfortable.\n\nLike, if a picture frame is crooked I might notice it, but then again I notice weird alignment in CAD drawings all day, so it’s hard to tell what’s just habit. My apartment isn’t, like, perfectly arranged or anything. My desk gets messy. I’ll straighten something sometimes, but I don’t feel stuck on it the way I… sorry, I’m kind of drifting.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that something you touch might be contaminated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with the wound area, I guess. Like even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll touch something or think I touched near it, and then this thought comes back that I missed one tiny spot or there’s still something there.\n\nAnd then my brain kind of jumps ahead to, what if that turns into an infection? Even if I can tell myself, no, I already cleaned it, I already did the steps… it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nIt’s kind of similar to work, weirdly, like reviewing a CAD file and then thinking I missed one tiny design issue that could hurt someone. Sorry, that’s not exactly touching contamination, but it’s the same kind of “one small thing I missed” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself washing your hands or cleaning things more than you think is necessary?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… though for me it kind of blends into checking, if that makes sense. It’s not just washing once and being done. I’ll clean or set out the dressing supplies, then I’ll think maybe I missed something, or touched the wrong part, and I end up going back through it again.\n\nAnd then it spreads to other things too—locks, work files, dimensions, revision notes. Sorry, that’s not exactly handwashing, but it’s the same pattern. I lose hours rechecking things because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in.\n\nI keep going back over the same things—like the dressing supplies, making sure I laid everything out right, then checking again because I’m not sure if I touched something wrong. And door locks too. Or at work, file dimensions and revision notes… I’ll tell myself I already checked it, but then I get this feeling like I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nIt’s not even that I find some new problem every time. It’s more like I have to go through the routine enough times until the anxiety finally drops. And then suddenly I’ve lost hours. I’ll sit there thinking, okay, one more pass, and then somehow it’s been the whole evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not like a picture exactly, more like a thought that cuts in and won’t leave.\n\nLike with the wound area, even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot and then it’s going to turn into an infection. And I try to tell myself that doesn’t make sense, or that I already did it, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nAnd at work it’s similar… like I’ll review a CAD file and then this thought pops up that I missed one small design mistake and somehow that could hurt someone. I know I’m probably going off into work stuff again, sorry, but it feels like the same thing. Like, nobody wants to be the person who misses some tiny dimension and then it causes a whole chain of problems.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where project deadlines are slipping because I stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit things. Not even improving them sometimes, just… checking until I feel okay enough to send it.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been missing paddleboarding meetups and workouts. I used to keep that time for myself, and now the routines just kind of eat it up. Then I look at the clock and it’s too late to go, or I’m too drained. So yeah, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or cleaning habits are related to making things symmetrical or exact in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about symmetry, exactly. It’s not like I need things lined up perfectly on a desk. It’s more that I lose hours rechecking because I can’t get that “okay, done” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like I’m scared there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and then I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet.\n\nSo I keep going back over things—my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?

And then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. Like I’ll review a CAD file and then suddenly it’s, what if I missed one tiny dimension and that turns into some design mistake that hurts someone? Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really hard to just talk myself out of it.

It’s not like checking once makes the thought go away. It keeps coming back.

What if that turns into an infection, or what if that little design mistake ends up hurting someone? Sorry, that’s not exactly touching contamination, but it’s the same kind of “one small thing I missed” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself washing your hands or cleaning things more than you think is necessary?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… though for me it kind of blends into checking, if that makes sense. It’s not just washing once and being done. I’ll clean or set out the dressing supplies, then I’ll think maybe I missed something, or touched the wrong part, and I end up going back through it again.\n\nAnd then it spreads to other things too—locks, work files, dimensions, revision notes. Sorry, that’s not exactly handwashing, but it’s the same pattern. I lose hours rechecking things because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s like I’m scared there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and then I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet.\n\nSo I keep going back over things—my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not always that I find something wrong. Most of the time I don’t. But I’ll still lose hours because I’m trying to get to that point where my anxiety drops enough and it feels… finished.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, like, at work they always tell you to be thorough, and in engineering that’s kind of drilled into you, but this feels past that. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check might be contaminated if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?\n\nAnd then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. Like I’ll review a CAD file and then suddenly it’s, what if I missed one tiny dimension and that turns into some design mistake that hurts someone? Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really hard to just talk myself out of it.\n\nIt’s not like checking once makes the thought go away. It keeps coming back.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the things you check could cause harm to yourself or others if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s kind of the main fear, I think. Even when I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny thing and then it gets infected?

And with work, it’s similar but not exactly the same. Like I’ll review a CAD file, and then I keep thinking, what if I missed one small dimension or detail and it turns into a design mistake that hurts someone? I mean, I know engineering has safety margins and reviews and all that—sorry, that’s probably beside the point—but in my head it feels like if I don’t check again, I’m being careless.

I can’t really just reason it away, even if part of me knows I already looked.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I’m losing a lot of time rechecking things. Like dressing supplies, door locks, even file dimensions and revision notes at work—I’m an engineer, so that part kind of spirals because everything is supposed to be precise, and I’ll think, “Well, one more look won’t hurt,” but then it’s not one more.\n\nI don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops a bit. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because hours can disappear, and I know I should just leave it alone, but I get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to check things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s less like I have one clear disaster in my head every time, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check. Like with the door lock, or my dressing supplies, or even file dimensions and revision notes at work—I’ll tell myself I just need to be sure, and then somehow I’m still there doing the same routine again.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m reviewing engineering drawings and you zoom in too far and suddenly every tiny line looks questionable, even if it was fine before. Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yes, I guess the fear is that if I don’t check, something could be wrong, and I won’t be able to relax until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. My roommate once joked that I alphabetized cables, which I didn’t—well, I sort of labeled them, but that was because we kept losing the HDMI one.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed needing to arrange things in a special way, not in the same way as the checking stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. Like I’ll be trying to work on something else, or even just move on with my day, and the thought cuts back in that I missed one tiny thing.\n\nIt’s usually after I already know I cleaned the wound area, or I already reviewed the CAD file, but then my brain goes, what if there’s still something there? What if that turns into an infection, or what if that little design mistake ends up hurting someone? And then I’m not really focused anymore—I’m just stuck on that image or possibility.\n\nIt’s annoying because I can sort of explain to myself that I already checked it, but it doesn’t actually make the thought leave. It’s like… I can reason it through, but it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not always like I’m picturing one exact catastrophe, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’m leaving something unsafe or wrong.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, or the door locks, or work stuff—file dimensions, revision notes—I’ll think, okay, just confirm it once, and then somehow I’m back in the same loop. It’s almost like debugging, except there’s no actual error message, just this feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, the checking is basically to prevent something bad, or to make the anxiety come down enough that I can leave it alone. And that can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like when things look neat, but I don’t think I need symmetry or that “just right” feeling to be comfortable.\n\nLike, if a picture frame is crooked I might notice it, but then again I notice weird alignment in CAD drawings all day, so it’s hard to tell what’s just habit. My apartment isn’t, like, perfectly arranged or anything. My desk gets messy. I’ll straighten something sometimes, but I don’t feel stuck on it the way I… sorry, I’m kind of drifting.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that something you touch might be contaminated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with the wound area, I guess. Like even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll touch something or think I touched near it, and then this thought comes back that I missed one tiny spot or there’s still something there.\n\nAnd then my brain kind of jumps ahead to, what if that turns into an infection? Even if I can tell myself, no, I already cleaned it, I already did the steps… it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nIt’s kind of similar to work, weirdly, like reviewing a CAD file and then thinking I missed one tiny design issue that could hurt someone. Sorry, that’s not exactly touching contamination, but it’s the same kind of “one small thing I missed” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself washing your hands or cleaning things more than you think is necessary?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… though for me it kind of blends into checking, if that makes sense. It’s not just washing once and being done. I’ll clean or set out the dressing supplies, then I’ll think maybe I missed something, or touched the wrong part, and I end up going back through it again.\n\nAnd then it spreads to other things too—locks, work files, dimensions, revision notes. Sorry, that’s not exactly handwashing, but it’s the same pattern. I lose hours rechecking things because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in.\n\nI keep going back over the same things—like the dressing supplies, making sure I laid everything out right, then checking again because I’m not sure if I touched something wrong. And door locks too. Or at work, file dimensions and revision notes… I’ll tell myself I already checked it, but then I get this feeling like I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nIt’s not even that I find some new problem every time. It’s more like I have to go through the routine enough times until the anxiety finally drops. And then suddenly I’ve lost hours. I’ll sit there thinking, okay, one more pass, and then somehow it’s been the whole evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not like a picture exactly, more like a thought that cuts in and won’t leave.\n\nLike with the wound area, even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot and then it’s going to turn into an infection. And I try to tell myself that doesn’t make sense, or that I already did it, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nAnd at work it’s similar… like I’ll review a CAD file and then this thought pops up that I missed one small design mistake and somehow that could hurt someone. I know I’m probably going off into work stuff again, sorry, but it feels like the same thing. I try to push it away or reason through it, and it just kind of comes back louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking habits are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kept thinking it was just me being careful, especially with engineering stuff, because you’re supposed to check your work, right? Like, nobody wants to be the person who misses some tiny dimension and then it causes a whole chain of problems.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where project deadlines are slipping because I stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit things. Not even improving them sometimes, just… checking until I feel okay enough to send it.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been missing paddleboarding meetups and workouts. I used to keep that time for myself, and now the routines just kind of eat it up. Then I look at the clock and it’s too late to go, or I’m too drained. So yeah, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or cleaning habits are related to making things symmetrical or exact in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about symmetry, exactly. I know I’m probably going off into work stuff again, sorry, but it feels like the same thing. I try to push it away or reason through it, and it just kind of comes back louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking habits are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kept thinking it was just me being careful, especially with engineering stuff, because you’re supposed to check your work, right? Like, nobody wants to be the person who misses some tiny dimension and then it causes a whole chain of problems.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where project deadlines are slipping because I stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit things. Not even improving them sometimes, just… checking until I feel okay enough to send it.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been missing paddleboarding meetups and workouts. I used to keep that time for myself, and now the routines just kind of eat it up. Then I look at the clock and it’s too late to go, or I’m too drained. So yeah, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or cleaning habits are related to making things symmetrical or exact in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about symmetry, exactly. It’s more like… exactness maybe, but not in a neatness way.\n\nLike with the dressing supplies, I’ll keep checking that everything is laid out right, or that I didn’t mess up some step, and then I’ll go back again because it doesn’t feel finished. Same with door locks, or file dimensions and revision notes at work. I know I already looked, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. It’s not like I need things lined up perfectly on a desk. It’s more that I lose hours rechecking because I can’t get that “okay, done” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like I’m scared there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and then I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet.\n\nSo I keep going back over things—my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not always that I find something wrong. Most of the time I don’t. But I’ll still lose hours because I’m trying to get to that point where my anxiety drops enough and it feels… finished.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, like, at work they always tell you to be thorough, and in engineering that’s kind of drilled into you, but this feels past that. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check might be contaminated if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?\n\nAnd then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. Like I’ll review a CAD file and then suddenly it’s, what if I missed one tiny dimension and that turns into some design mistake that hurts someone? Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really hard to just talk myself out of it.\n\nIt’s not like checking once makes the thought go away. It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check could cause harm to yourself or others if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the main fear, I think. Even when I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny thing and then it gets infected?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar but not exactly the same. Like I’ll review a CAD file, and then I keep thinking, what if I missed one small dimension or detail and it turns into a design mistake that hurts someone? I mean, I know engineering has safety margins and reviews and all that—sorry, that’s probably beside the point—but in my head it feels like if I don’t check again, I’m being careless.\n\nI can’t really just reason it away, even if part of me knows I already looked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, I mean… I do believe it in the moment. Even if another part of me knows I already checked.

Like with the wound cleaning, I can see that I cleaned it, but then this thought keeps cutting in like, what if there’s one tiny spot I missed and now that becomes an infection? Yeah… in the moment, I do. Like even if I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny bit and it turns into an infection?

I know that sounds like a big jump — and honestly sometimes I’ll get stuck on the smallest thing, like a number on the screen, and then I’m thinking about consequences way past what I’m actually looking at.

So… yeah. It feels like if I don’t check again, something bad could happen, even though part of me knows I’ve already checked and should be able to move on.

It’s like… I can reason it through, but it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not always like I’m picturing one exact catastrophe, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’m leaving something unsafe or wrong.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, or the door locks, or work stuff—file dimensions, revision notes—I’ll think, okay, just confirm it once, and then somehow I’m back in the same loop. It’s almost like debugging, except there’s no actual error message, just this feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, the checking is basically to prevent something bad, or to make the anxiety come down enough that I can leave it alone. And that can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like when things look neat, but I don’t think I need symmetry or that “just right” feeling to be comfortable.\n\nLike, if a picture frame is crooked I might notice it, but then again I notice weird alignment in CAD drawings all day, so it’s hard to tell what’s just habit. My apartment isn’t, like, perfectly arranged or anything. My desk gets messy. I’ll straighten something sometimes, but I don’t feel stuck on it the way I… sorry, I’m kind of drifting.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that something you touch might be contaminated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with the wound area, I guess. Like even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll touch something or think I touched near it, and then this thought comes back that I missed one tiny spot or there’s still something there.\n\nAnd then my brain kind of jumps ahead to, what if that turns into an infection? Even if I can tell myself, no, I already cleaned it, I already did the steps… it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nIt’s kind of similar to work, weirdly, like reviewing a CAD file and then thinking I missed one tiny design issue that could hurt someone. Sorry, that’s not exactly touching contamination, but it’s the same kind of “one small thing I missed” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself washing your hands or cleaning things more than you think is necessary?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… though for me it kind of blends into checking, if that makes sense. It’s not just washing once and being done. I’ll clean or set out the dressing supplies, then I’ll think maybe I missed something, or touched the wrong part, and I end up going back through it again.\n\nAnd then it spreads to other things too—locks, work files, dimensions, revision notes. Sorry, that’s not exactly handwashing, but it’s the same pattern. I lose hours rechecking things because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in.\n\nI keep going back over the same things—like the dressing supplies, making sure I laid everything out right, then checking again because I’m not sure if I touched something wrong. And door locks too. Or at work, file dimensions and revision notes… I’ll tell myself I already checked it, but then I get this feeling like I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nIt’s not even that I find some new problem every time. It’s more like I have to go through the routine enough times until the anxiety finally drops. And then suddenly I’ve lost hours. I’ll sit there thinking, okay, one more pass, and then somehow it’s been the whole evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not like a picture exactly, more like a thought that cuts in and won’t leave.\n\nLike with the wound area, even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot and then it’s going to turn into an infection. And I try to tell myself that doesn’t make sense, or that I already did it, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nAnd at work it’s similar… like I’ll review a CAD file and then this thought pops up that I missed one small design mistake and somehow that could hurt someone. I know I’m probably going off into work stuff again, sorry, but it feels like the same thing. I try to push it away or reason through it, and it just kind of comes back louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking habits are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kept thinking it was just me being careful, especially with engineering stuff, because you’re supposed to check your work, right? Like, nobody wants to be the person who misses some tiny dimension and then it causes a whole chain of problems.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where project deadlines are slipping because I stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit things. Not even improving them sometimes, just… checking until I feel okay enough to send it.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been missing paddleboarding meetups and workouts. I used to keep that time for myself, and now the routines just kind of eat it up. Then I look at the clock and it’s too late to go, or I’m too drained. So yeah, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or cleaning habits are related to making things symmetrical or exact in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about symmetry, exactly. It’s more like… exactness maybe, but not in a neatness way.\n\nLike with the dressing supplies, I’ll keep checking that everything is laid out right, or that I didn’t mess up some step, and then I’ll go back again because it doesn’t feel finished. Same with door locks, or file dimensions and revision notes at work. I know I already looked, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. It’s not like I need things lined up perfectly on a desk. It’s more that I lose hours rechecking because I can’t get that “okay, done” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like I’m scared there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and then I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet.\n\nSo I keep going back over things—my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not always that I find something wrong. Most of the time I don’t. But I’ll still lose hours because I’m trying to get to that point where my anxiety drops enough and it feels… finished.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, like, at work they always tell you to be thorough, and in engineering that’s kind of drilled into you, but this feels past that. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check might be contaminated if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?\n\nAnd then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. Like I’ll review a CAD file and then suddenly it’s, what if I missed one tiny dimension and that turns into some design mistake that hurts someone? Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really hard to just talk myself out of it.\n\nIt’s not like checking once makes the thought go away. It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check could cause harm to yourself or others if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the main fear, I think. Even when I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny thing and then it gets infected?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar but not exactly the same. Like I’ll review a CAD file, and then I keep thinking, what if I missed one small dimension or detail and it turns into a design mistake that hurts someone? I mean, I know engineering has safety margins and reviews and all that—sorry, that’s probably beside the point—but in my head it feels like if I don’t check again, I’m being careless.\n\nI can’t really just reason it away, even if part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that not checking things repeatedly would put yourself or others at risk of harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do believe it in the moment. Even if another part of me knows I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound cleaning, I can see that I cleaned it, but then this thought keeps cutting in like, what if there’s one tiny spot I missed and now that becomes an infection? And then with CAD files, it’s not the same thing, but it feels similar—like if I missed one small detail, one dimension, it could turn into some design mistake that hurts someone.\n\nI know that sounds like I’m making this big chain of events out of one little thing. I do that a lot, honestly. I’ll start thinking about one measurement and then suddenly I’m picturing consequences way beyond the file in front of me. But when the thought is there, I can’t just reason it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't check things repeatedly, something bad would happen to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… in the moment, I do. Like even if I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny bit and it turns into an infection?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar. I’ll have reviewed a CAD file already, but then my mind kind of latches onto one possible tiny detail, like a dimension, and suddenly I’m thinking, what if that becomes some design mistake that hurts someone. I know that sounds like a big jump — and honestly sometimes I’ll get stuck on the smallest thing, like a number on the screen, and then I’m thinking about consequences way past what I’m actually looking at.\n\nSo… yeah. It feels like if I don’t check again, something bad could happen, even though part of me knows I’ve already checked and should be able to move on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly fear of making a mistake, I think… though the dressing supplies part feels a little like contamination too, because I’m worried something isn’t clean enough.

But what really brings me in is I’m losing hours to rechecking. Like I’ll check the dressing stuff, then door locks, then at work it’s file dimensions or revision notes, and I just don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.

Sorry, I know you asked either mistake or contamination — it’s kind of both depending on the thing, but the repeated checking is the problem.

It’s embarrassing, honestly, because hours can disappear, and I know I should just leave it alone, but I get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to check things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s less like I have one clear disaster in my head every time, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check. Like with the door lock, or my dressing supplies, or even file dimensions and revision notes at work—I’ll tell myself I just need to be sure, and then somehow I’m still there doing the same routine again.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m reviewing engineering drawings and you zoom in too far and suddenly every tiny line looks questionable, even if it was fine before. Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. My roommate once joked that I alphabetized cables, which I didn’t—well, I sort of labeled them, but that was because we kept losing the HDMI one.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed needing to arrange things in a special way, not in the same way as the checking stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens. Like I’ll be trying to work on something else, or even just move on with my day, and the thought cuts back in that I missed one tiny thing.\n\nIt’s usually after I already know I cleaned the wound area, or I already reviewed the CAD file, but then my brain goes, what if there’s still something there? What if that turns into an infection, or what if that little design mistake ends up hurting someone? And then I’m not really focused anymore—I’m just stuck on that image or possibility.\n\nIt’s annoying because I can sort of explain to myself that I already checked it, but it doesn’t actually make the thought leave. It’s like… I can reason it through, but it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not always like I’m picturing one exact catastrophe, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’m leaving something unsafe or wrong.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, or the door locks, or work stuff—file dimensions, revision notes—I’ll think, okay, just confirm it once, and then somehow I’m back in the same loop. It’s almost like debugging, except there’s no actual error message, just this feeling that I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, the checking is basically to prevent something bad, or to make the anxiety come down enough that I can leave it alone. And that can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be just right or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like when things look neat, but I don’t think I need symmetry or that “just right” feeling to be comfortable.\n\nLike, if a picture frame is crooked I might notice it, but then again I notice weird alignment in CAD drawings all day, so it’s hard to tell what’s just habit. My apartment isn’t, like, perfectly arranged or anything. My desk gets messy. I’ll straighten something sometimes, but I don’t feel stuck on it the way I… sorry, I’m kind of drifting.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that something you touch might be contaminated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with the wound area, I guess. Like even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll touch something or think I touched near it, and then this thought comes back that I missed one tiny spot or there’s still something there.\n\nAnd then my brain kind of jumps ahead to, what if that turns into an infection? Even if I can tell myself, no, I already cleaned it, I already did the steps… it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nIt’s kind of similar to work, weirdly, like reviewing a CAD file and then thinking I missed one tiny design issue that could hurt someone. Sorry, that’s not exactly touching contamination, but it’s the same kind of “one small thing I missed” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself washing your hands or cleaning things more than you think is necessary?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… though for me it kind of blends into checking, if that makes sense. It’s not just washing once and being done. I’ll clean or set out the dressing supplies, then I’ll think maybe I missed something, or touched the wrong part, and I end up going back through it again.\n\nAnd then it spreads to other things too—locks, work files, dimensions, revision notes. Sorry, that’s not exactly handwashing, but it’s the same pattern. I lose hours rechecking things because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in.\n\nI keep going back over the same things—like the dressing supplies, making sure I laid everything out right, then checking again because I’m not sure if I touched something wrong. And door locks too. Or at work, file dimensions and revision notes… I’ll tell myself I already checked it, but then I get this feeling like I’m not allowed to move on yet.\n\nIt’s not even that I find some new problem every time. It’s more like I have to go through the routine enough times until the anxiety finally drops. And then suddenly I’ve lost hours. I’ll sit there thinking, okay, one more pass, and then somehow it’s been the whole evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not like a picture exactly, more like a thought that cuts in and won’t leave.\n\nLike with the wound area, even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot and then it’s going to turn into an infection. And I try to tell myself that doesn’t make sense, or that I already did it, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nAnd at work it’s similar… like I’ll review a CAD file and then this thought pops up that I missed one small design mistake and somehow that could hurt someone. I know I’m probably going off into work stuff again, sorry, but it feels like the same thing. I try to push it away or reason through it, and it just kind of comes back louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking habits are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kept thinking it was just me being careful, especially with engineering stuff, because you’re supposed to check your work, right? Like, nobody wants to be the person who misses some tiny dimension and then it causes a whole chain of problems.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where project deadlines are slipping because I stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit things. Not even improving them sometimes, just… checking until I feel okay enough to send it.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been missing paddleboarding meetups and workouts. I used to keep that time for myself, and now the routines just kind of eat it up. Then I look at the clock and it’s too late to go, or I’m too drained. So yeah, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or cleaning habits are related to making things symmetrical or exact in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about symmetry, exactly. It’s more like… exactness maybe, but not in a neatness way.\n\nLike with the dressing supplies, I’ll keep checking that everything is laid out right, or that I didn’t mess up some step, and then I’ll go back again because it doesn’t feel finished. Same with door locks, or file dimensions and revision notes at work. I know I already looked, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. It’s not like I need things lined up perfectly on a desk. It’s more that I lose hours rechecking because I can’t get that “okay, done” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like I’m scared there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and then I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet.\n\nSo I keep going back over things—my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not always that I find something wrong. Most of the time I don’t. But I’ll still lose hours because I’m trying to get to that point where my anxiety drops enough and it feels… finished.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, like, at work they always tell you to be thorough, and in engineering that’s kind of drilled into you, but this feels past that. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check might be contaminated if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?\n\nAnd then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. Like I’ll review a CAD file and then suddenly it’s, what if I missed one tiny dimension and that turns into some design mistake that hurts someone? Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really hard to just talk myself out of it.\n\nIt’s not like checking once makes the thought go away. It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check could cause harm to yourself or others if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the main fear, I think. Even when I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny thing and then it gets infected?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar but not exactly the same. I’ll sit there thinking, okay, one more pass, and then somehow it’s been the whole evening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not like a picture exactly, more like a thought that cuts in and won’t leave.\n\nLike with the wound area, even after I know I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot and then it’s going to turn into an infection. And I try to tell myself that doesn’t make sense, or that I already did it, but it doesn’t really stick.\n\nAnd at work it’s similar… like I’ll review a CAD file and then this thought pops up that I missed one small design mistake and somehow that could hurt someone. I know I’m probably going off into work stuff again, sorry, but it feels like the same thing. I try to push it away or reason through it, and it just kind of comes back louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking habits are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kept thinking it was just me being careful, especially with engineering stuff, because you’re supposed to check your work, right? Like, nobody wants to be the person who misses some tiny dimension and then it causes a whole chain of problems.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where project deadlines are slipping because I stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit things. Not even improving them sometimes, just… checking until I feel okay enough to send it.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been missing paddleboarding meetups and workouts. I used to keep that time for myself, and now the routines just kind of eat it up. Then I look at the clock and it’s too late to go, or I’m too drained. So yeah, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or cleaning habits are related to making things symmetrical or exact in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about symmetry, exactly. It’s more like… exactness maybe, but not in a neatness way.\n\nLike with the dressing supplies, I’ll keep checking that everything is laid out right, or that I didn’t mess up some step, and then I’ll go back again because it doesn’t feel finished. Same with door locks, or file dimensions and revision notes at work. I know I already looked, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. It’s not like I need things lined up perfectly on a desk. It’s more that I lose hours rechecking because I can’t get that “okay, done” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like I’m scared there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and then I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet.\n\nSo I keep going back over things—my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not always that I find something wrong. Most of the time I don’t. But I’ll still lose hours because I’m trying to get to that point where my anxiety drops enough and it feels… finished.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, like, at work they always tell you to be thorough, and in engineering that’s kind of drilled into you, but this feels past that. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check might be contaminated if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?\n\nAnd then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. Like I’ll review a CAD file and then suddenly it’s, what if I missed one tiny dimension and that turns into some design mistake that hurts someone? Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really hard to just talk myself out of it.\n\nIt’s not like checking once makes the thought go away. It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check could cause harm to yourself or others if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the main fear, I think. Even when I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny thing and then it gets infected?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar but not exactly the same. Like I’ll review a CAD file, and then I keep thinking, what if I missed one small dimension or detail and it turns into a design mistake that hurts someone? I mean, I know engineering has safety margins and reviews and all that—sorry, that’s probably beside the point—but in my head it feels like if I don’t check again, I’m being careless.\n\nI can’t really just reason it away, even if part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that not checking things repeatedly would put yourself or others at risk of harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do believe it in the moment. Even if another part of me knows I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound cleaning, I can see that I cleaned it, but then this thought keeps cutting in like, what if there’s one tiny spot I missed and now that becomes an infection? And then with CAD files, it’s not the same thing, but it feels similar—like if I missed one small detail, one dimension, it could turn into some design mistake that hurts someone.\n\nI know that sounds like I’m making this big chain of events out of one little thing. I do that a lot, honestly. I’ll start thinking about one measurement and then suddenly I’m picturing consequences way beyond the file in front of me. But when the thought is there, I can’t just reason it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't check things repeatedly, something bad would happen to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… in the moment, I do. Like I’ll check the dressing stuff, then door locks, then at work it’s file dimensions or revision notes, and I just don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nSorry, I know you asked either mistake or contamination — it’s kind of both depending on the thing, but the repeated checking is the problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But I’ll still lose hours because I’m trying to get to that point where my anxiety drops enough and it feels… finished.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, like, at work they always tell you to be thorough, and in engineering that’s kind of drilled into you, but this feels past that. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check might be contaminated if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?\n\nAnd then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. Like I’ll review a CAD file and then suddenly it’s, what if I missed one tiny dimension and that turns into some design mistake that hurts someone? It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check could cause harm to yourself or others if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the main fear, I think. Even when I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny thing and then it gets infected?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar but not exactly the same. Like I’ll review a CAD file, and then I keep thinking, what if I missed one small dimension or detail and it turns into a design mistake that hurts someone? I mean, I know engineering has safety margins and reviews and all that—sorry, that’s probably beside the point—but in my head it feels like if I don’t check again, I’m being careless.\n\nI can’t really just reason it away, even if part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that not checking things repeatedly would put yourself or others at risk of harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do believe it in the moment. But when the thought is there, I can’t just reason it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't check things repeatedly, something bad would happen to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… in the moment, I do. Like even if I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny bit and it turns into an infection?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar. And it’s not even that I find something most of the time, it’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that my anxiety finally drops.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your need to repeatedly check things mostly driven by a fear of making a mistake?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things—my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, mostly. I mean, it’s not always some huge mistake, but that’s the feeling underneath it—like if I don’t recheck, I might miss something.

What’s been getting bad is just… the time. I’m losing hours going back over dressing supplies, door locks, CAD dimensions, revision notes. And then I’ll think of something random like, did I actually look at that one line, or was I just staring at it? It reminds me of when I was in school and I’d reread the same paragraph over and over before exams, except now it’s not just studying, it’s everything.

So yeah, I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety drops a bit.

It’s embarrassing, honestly, because hours can disappear, and I know I should just leave it alone, but I get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to check things because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s less like I have one clear disaster in my head every time, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check. Sorry, that’s not exactly touching contamination, but it’s the same kind of “one small thing I missed” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself washing your hands or cleaning things more than you think is necessary?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… though for me it kind of blends into checking, if that makes sense. It’s not just washing once and being done. I’ll clean or set out the dressing supplies, then I’ll think maybe I missed something, or touched the wrong part, and I end up going back through it again.\n\nAnd then it spreads to other things too—locks, work files, dimensions, revision notes. Sorry, that’s not exactly handwashing, but it’s the same pattern. I lose hours rechecking things because I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to perform over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check might be contaminated if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?\n\nAnd then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check could cause harm to yourself or others if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the main fear, I think. Even if another part of me knows I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound cleaning, I can see that I cleaned it, but then this thought keeps cutting in like, what if there’s one tiny spot I missed and now that becomes an infection? And then with CAD files, it’s not the same thing, but it feels similar—like if I missed one small detail, one dimension, it could turn into some design mistake that hurts someone.\n\nI know that sounds like I’m making this big chain of events out of one little thing. I do that a lot, honestly. I’ll start thinking about one measurement and then suddenly I’m picturing consequences way beyond the file in front of me. But when the thought is there, I can’t just reason it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't check things repeatedly, something bad would happen to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… in the moment, I do. Like even if I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny bit and it turns into an infection?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar. I’ll have reviewed a CAD file already, but then my mind kind of latches onto one possible tiny detail, like a dimension, and suddenly I’m thinking, what if that becomes some design mistake that hurts someone. I know that sounds like a big jump — and honestly sometimes I’ll get stuck on the smallest thing, like a number on the screen, and then I’m thinking about consequences way past what I’m actually looking at.\n\nSo… yeah. It feels like if I don’t check again, something bad could happen, even though part of me knows I’ve already checked and should be able to move on.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to repeatedly check things mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake or because you think something might be contaminated?\n\nPatient: Mostly fear of making a mistake, I think… though the dressing supplies part feels a little like contamination too, because I’m worried something isn’t clean enough.\n\nBut what really brings me in is I’m losing hours to rechecking.
