I know I’m probably going off into work stuff again, sorry, but it feels like the same thing. I try to push it away or reason through it, and it just kind of comes back louder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking habits are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I kept thinking it was just me being careful, especially with engineering stuff, because you’re supposed to check your work, right? Like, nobody wants to be the person who misses some tiny dimension and then it causes a whole chain of problems.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where project deadlines are slipping because I stay late trying to get comfortable enough to actually submit things. Not even improving them sometimes, just… checking until I feel okay enough to send it.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been missing paddleboarding meetups and workouts. I used to keep that time for myself, and now the routines just kind of eat it up. Then I look at the clock and it’s too late to go, or I’m too drained. So yeah, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or cleaning habits are related to making things symmetrical or exact in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about symmetry, exactly. It’s more like… exactness maybe, but not in a neatness way.\n\nLike with the dressing supplies, I’ll keep checking that everything is laid out right, or that I didn’t mess up some step, and then I’ll go back again because it doesn’t feel finished. Same with door locks, or file dimensions and revision notes at work. I know I already looked, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. It’s not like I need things lined up perfectly on a desk. It’s more that I lose hours rechecking because I can’t get that “okay, done” feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like I’m scared there’s one mistake I didn’t catch, and then I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on yet.\n\nSo I keep going back over things—my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions, revision notes. It’s not always that I find something wrong. Most of the time I don’t. But I’ll still lose hours because I’m trying to get to that point where my anxiety drops enough and it feels… finished.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, like, at work they always tell you to be thorough, and in engineering that’s kind of drilled into you, but this feels past that. It’s the same routine over and over until I can finally stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check might be contaminated if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… not everything I check feels contaminated, but with the wound stuff, that’s definitely part of it. Even after I know I cleaned the area, this thought cuts in like, what if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?\n\nAnd then my brain does the same kind of thing at work, even though that’s not contamination exactly. Like I’ll review a CAD file and then suddenly it’s, what if I missed one tiny dimension and that turns into some design mistake that hurts someone? Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really hard to just talk myself out of it.\n\nIt’s not like checking once makes the thought go away. It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check could cause harm to yourself or others if you don't check them repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the main fear, I think. Even when I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny thing and then it gets infected?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar but not exactly the same. Like I’ll review a CAD file, and then I keep thinking, what if I missed one small dimension or detail and it turns into a design mistake that hurts someone? I mean, I know engineering has safety margins and reviews and all that—sorry, that’s probably beside the point—but in my head it feels like if I don’t check again, I’m being careless.\n\nI can’t really just reason it away, even if part of me knows I already looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that not checking things repeatedly would put yourself or others at risk of harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do believe it in the moment. Even if another part of me knows I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound cleaning, I can see that I cleaned it, but then this thought keeps cutting in like, what if there’s one tiny spot I missed and now that becomes an infection? And then with CAD files, it’s not the same thing, but it feels similar—like if I missed one small detail, one dimension, it could turn into some design mistake that hurts someone.\n\nI know that sounds like I’m making this big chain of events out of one little thing. I do that a lot, honestly. I’ll start thinking about one measurement and then suddenly I’m picturing consequences way beyond the file in front of me. But when the thought is there, I can’t just reason it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't check things repeatedly, something bad would happen to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… in the moment, I do. Like even if I know I already cleaned the wound area, the thought comes back like, what if I missed one tiny bit and it turns into an infection?\n\nAnd with work, it’s similar. I’ll have reviewed a CAD file already, but then my mind kind of latches onto one possible tiny detail, like a dimension, and suddenly I’m thinking, what if that becomes some design mistake that hurts someone. I know that sounds like a big jump — and honestly sometimes I’ll get stuck on the smallest thing, like a number on the screen, and then I’m thinking about consequences way past what I’m actually looking at.\n\nSo… yeah. It feels like if I don’t check again, something bad could happen, even though part of me knows I’ve already checked and should be able to move on.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to repeatedly check things mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake or because you think something might be contaminated?\n\nPatient: Mostly fear of making a mistake, I think… though the dressing supplies part feels a little like contamination too, because I’m worried something isn’t clean enough.\n\nBut what really brings me in is I’m losing hours to rechecking. Like I’ll check the dressing stuff, then door locks, then at work it’s file dimensions or revision notes, and I just don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops.\n\nSorry, I know you asked either mistake or contamination — it’s kind of both depending on the thing, but the repeated checking is the problem.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to repeatedly check things more about being afraid of missing something or more about being afraid of something being dirty or contaminated?\n\nPatient: More afraid of missing something, I think. Like… the dirty or contaminated thing is there with the dressing supplies, but the bigger pattern is I’m scared I missed a step or a detail.\n\nI’ll get stuck rechecking the dressing stuff, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—sometimes it’s like hours disappear. And it’s not even that I find something most of the time, it’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that my anxiety finally drops.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to repeatedly check things mostly driven by a fear of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. I mean, it’s not always some huge mistake, but that’s the feeling underneath it—like if I don’t recheck, I might miss something.\n\nWhat’s been getting bad is just… the time. I’m losing hours going back over dressing supplies, door locks, CAD dimensions, revision notes. And then I’ll think of something random like, did I actually look at that one line, or was I just staring at it? It reminds me of when I was in school and I’d reread the same paragraph over and over before exams, except now it’s not just studying, it’s everything.\n\nSo yeah, I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times and the anxiety drops a bit."
